To control the growth test locally as $x$ varies, we track possible zeros of $m_x(v^{D+1})-v$. By <a href="#eq:minimum-root" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:minimum-root">[eq:minimum-root]</a>, each such zero forces $Q(x,v^{D+1},v)=0$. Define the controlled polynomial $$\label{eq:degree-polynomial}
 E(x,v)=Q(x,v^{D+1},v)=\sum_{j=0}^{d_E}E_j(x)v^j,$$ where $d_E$ is a short, polynomial-time computable degree bound. Coefficient extraction makes the $E_j$ a uniformly controlled family. Crucially, $$\label{eq:nonzero-degree-polynomial}
 E_D(x)=1\qquad\text{for every }x.$$ Indeed, the term $T^D$ in $Q$ gives $v^D$. Every other term has $T$-degree at most $D-1$, so after substitution its $v$-exponent has the form $a(D+1)+b$ with $a\ge0$ and $0\le b<D$; none equals $D$. Thus $E(x,\cdot)$ is never the zero polynomial. Fixing its actual degree keeps its leading coefficient locally away from zero, and hence gives a locally uniform bound on its roots.

<a id="stmt-prop-degree-pieces"></a>
<div id="prop:degree-pieces" class="proposition">

**Proposition 23** (Local constancy on degree pieces). *For $0\le d\le d_E$, let $$A_d=\{x\in\mathbb R^r:E_d(x)\ne0,\ E_j(x)=0\text{ for }j>d\}.$$ The sets $A_d$ partition $\mathbb R^r$. Within each $A_d$, the good parameters form a subset that is both open and closed in the relative topology.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-degree-pieces"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-degree-pieces</summary>

*Proof.* The partition assertion follows from <a href="#eq:nonzero-degree-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nonzero-degree-polynomial">[eq:nonzero-degree-polynomial]</a>. Fix $x_0\in A_d$. On a neighborhood $V$ of $x_0$ relative to $A_d$, continuity bounds $|E_d|$ below by a positive number and all the other coefficient moduli above. The elementary leading-term bound therefore gives $R>0$ such that $$E(x,v)\ne0\qquad(x\in V,\ v\ge R).$$ Consequently the continuous function $m_x(v^{D+1})-v$ never vanishes on this range: a zero would contradict <a href="#eq:minimum-root" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:minimum-root">[eq:minimum-root]</a> and <a href="#eq:degree-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degree-polynomial">[eq:degree-polynomial]</a>. For each $x\in V$ its sign is constant as $v$ varies in $[R,\infty)$, and <a href="#eq:growth-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:growth-test">[eq:growth-test]</a> identifies that sign with goodness. By continuity in $x$, its nonzero sign at $v=R$ is constant on a smaller relative neighborhood of $x_0$. Goodness is therefore locally constant on $A_d$, which proves both relative openness and relative closedness. ◻

</details>

The sets $A_d$ need not be closed in the ambient space. To make the separation in Proposition <a href="#prop:degree-pieces" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:degree-pieces">23</a> usable in a compact minimization argument, introduce one real variable $t$ and define <a id="eq:closed-lift"></a>$$\label{eq:closed-lift}
 W_d(x,t)=(E_d(x)t-1)^2+\sum_{j=d+1}^{d_E}E_j(x)^2,
 \qquad
 Z_d=\{(x,t)\in\mathbb R^{r+1}:W_d(x,t)=0\}.$$ The $W_d$ are nonnegative controlled polynomials, uniformly in the short index $d$. The set $Z_d$ is closed, and projection onto $x$ is a homeomorphism from $Z_d$ onto $A_d$, with inverse $x\mapsto(x,1/E_d(x))$. Thus the good part of $Z_d$ and its complement in $Z_d$ are both closed in the ambient Euclidean space. We have separated the two behaviors without needing a description of the connected components of a degree piece.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Algebraic coordinates in a selected closed part

We now show that a nonnegative polynomial $W$ supplies coordinate polynomials whose roots include the coordinates of a point in every nonempty part of $\{W=0\}$ that is both open and closed within that zero set. Their construction is independent of which part is selected and requires no equations describing that part. The use of a deformation and limiting characteristic-polynomial roots is related to the sampling methods in Basu, Pollack, and Roy (Basu, Pollack, and Roy 2006, vol. 10, secs. 12.5–12.6) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[BasuPollackRoy2006](#ref-BasuPollackRoy2006)]. The argument below proves the precise form needed here directly.

<a id="stmt-prop-candidate-coordinates"></a>
<div id="prop:candidate-coordinates" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:candidate-relations"></a>
**Proposition 24** (Candidate coordinates). *Let $W\in\mathbb R[c_1,\ldots,c_k]$ be nonnegative on $\mathbb R^k$, where $k\ge1$, and let $C$ be a nonempty subset of $Z=\{W=0\}$ that is both open and closed in $Z$. Choose an odd integer $\ell\ge3$ with $\ell>\deg W$, and put $$G(c)=\sum_{i=1}^k c_i^{\ell+1},\qquad
 H_w(c)=G(c)+(\ell+1)wW(c).$$ In the quotient by $$\label{eq:candidate-relations}
 c_i^\ell+w\frac{\partial W}{\partial c_i}=0
 \qquad(1\le i\le k),$$ form the coordinate multiplication polynomials <a id="eq:coordinate-polynomials"></a>$$\label{eq:coordinate-polynomials}
 P_i(w,T)=\det(TI-M_{c_i})=\sum_j P_{i,j}(T)w^j.$$ For each $i$, let $j_i$ be the largest index for which $P_{i,j_i}\ne0$. There exists $c^*\in C$ such that $$P_{i,j_i}(c_i^*)=0\qquad(1\le i\le k).$$ If $W$ is a controlled family of integer polynomials, $k$ is polynomially bounded, and $\ell$ is short and computable in polynomial time, then the polynomials $P_i$ and $P_{i,j}$ are uniformly controlled.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-candidate-coordinates"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-candidate-coordinates</summary>

*Proof.* The relations <a href="#eq:candidate-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:candidate-relations">[eq:candidate-relations]</a> satisfy the degree hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:monomial-basis" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:monomial-basis">9</a>, with coefficient ring $\mathbb R[w]$. Thus the coordinate multiplication matrices exist, and each $P_i$ is monic of degree $\ell^k$ in $T$. In particular, $P_i\ne0$ and $j_i$ exists. For controlled integer families, Proposition <a href="#prop:multiplication-determinant" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:multiplication-determinant">13</a> and coefficient extraction give the final assertion.

Fix $c^0\in C$ and choose $M>G(c^0)$. The set $$K_0=\{c:G(c)\le M\}$$ is compact. Since $Z$ is closed and $C$ is relatively both open and closed, the sets $$S_g=C\cap K_0,\qquad S_b=(Z\setminus C)\cap K_0$$ are disjoint compact sets, and $c^0\in S_g$. Choose $\epsilon>0$ so that the compact set <a id="eq:compact-neighborhood"></a>$$\label{eq:compact-neighborhood}
 K=\{c\in K_0:\operatorname{dist}(c,S_g)\le\epsilon\}$$ is disjoint from $S_b$. This is possible because disjoint nonempty compact sets have positive distance; if $S_b$ is empty, any positive $\epsilon$ suffices. Figure <a href="#fig:compact-neighborhood" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:compact-neighborhood">2</a> indicates the two boundaries whose role we must control.

For a sequence $w_\nu>0$ tending to $+\infty$, let $c^\nu$ minimize $H_{w_\nu}$ on $K$. Since $c^0\in K$ and $W(c^0)=0$, nonnegativity gives <a id="eq:penalty-bounds"></a>$$\label{eq:penalty-bounds}
 G(c^\nu)+(\ell+1)w_\nu W(c^\nu)
 \le G(c^0)<M.$$ After passage to a subsequence, compactness gives $c^\nu\to c^*\in K$. The same bound forces $W(c^\nu)\to0$, so $c^*\in Z\cap K$. Because $K\cap S_b=\varnothing$, we have $c^*\in S_g\subset C$. Furthermore, $$G(c^*)\le G(c^0)<M,
 \qquad \operatorname{dist}(c^*,S_g)=0<\epsilon.$$ Both inequalities defining $K$ are strict at $c^*$, so $c^*$ is an interior point of $K$. Hence the convergent minimizers $c^\nu$ are also interior for all sufficiently large $\nu$. They satisfy the unconstrained critical equations <a href="#eq:candidate-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:candidate-relations">[eq:candidate-relations]</a>. Evaluation at these common zeros gives $$P_i(w_\nu,c_i^\nu)=0.$$ Divide by $w_\nu^{j_i}$. The lower powers tend to zero because $c_i^\nu$ stays bounded, and passage to the limit yields $P_{i,j_i}(c_i^*)=0$, as required. ◻

</details>

<figure id="fig:compact-neighborhood">

<figcaption>The compact-neighborhood argument, schematically. The selected zero set $S_{g}$ and the excluded zero set $S_{b}$ are separated inside $K_{0}$. The set $K$ can meet the boundary $G = M$. Penalized minimizers converge to $c^{*} ∈ S_{g}$ with $G(c^{*}) < M$ and distance zero from $S_{g}$, strictly below $ϵ$. They are therefore eventually interior to $K$, away from both boundary portions. The drawn shapes assert no smoothness or connectedness of these sets.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-compact-neighborhood"></a>

   and (2.6,1.1) .. (3.2,1.65);

Inside $K_0$, a region $K$ intersects the boundary $G = M$. Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:candidate-coordinates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:candidate-coordinates">24</a> to $W=W_d$ and to the good part of $Z_d$. We use $c=(x,t)\in\mathbb R^{r+1}$ and choose $\ell$ from a uniform short degree bound on $W_d$. Whenever this good part is nonempty, it contains a point whose coordinates are roots of the nonzero coefficient polynomials $P_{i,j_i}$. These polynomials are controlled uniformly in $d,i,j_i$.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### The final decision

<a id="eq:selected-coordinates"></a>
<a id="eq:final-nonempty"></a>
<a id="eq:final-no-counterexample"></a>
We can now use root labels to replace the real choice of $x$ by short discrete data. Choose an integer $0\le d\le d_E$ and, for each $1\le i\le r+1$, an index $j_i$ within a uniform short bound on the $w$-degree of $P_i$. For each $P_{i,j_i}$ choose root-label data as in Proposition <a href="#prop:root-labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:root-labels">14</a>, with the auxiliary positivity test equal to $1$, and write $L_i$ for the resulting controlled polynomial. Let $\mathcal S(c)$ denote the conjunction $$\label{eq:selected-coordinates}
 W_d(c)=0,\qquad
 P_{i,j_i}(c_i)=0,\qquad L_i(c_i)>0
 \quad(1\le i\le r+1).$$ For these choices, accept exactly when both statements hold: $$\begin{aligned}
 &\exists c\in\mathbb R^{r+1}\;\mathcal S(c),
 \label{eq:final-nonempty}\\
 &\neg\exists(c,z)\in\mathbb R^{r+1+n}\;
       \bigl(\mathcal S(c)\ \wedge\ F(x,z)=0\bigr).
 \label{eq:final-no-counterexample}
\end{aligned}$$ Each existential statement is an instance of Theorem <a href="#thm:controlled-existential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:controlled-existential">18</a>. Their conjunction, with negation as displayed, is therefore an available decision.

We verify the two directions separately; they use the labels in different ways. If the test accepts, choose $c=(x,t)$ from <a href="#eq:final-nonempty" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-nonempty">[eq:final-nonempty]</a>. Statement <a href="#eq:final-no-counterexample" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-no-counterexample">[eq:final-no-counterexample]</a> implies that $F(x,\cdot)$ has no real zero. Thus $x$ is good. This implication holds for every choice of the indices and labels, even if a selected coefficient polynomial is zero or the conjunction selects more than one point.

Conversely, suppose a good $x$ exists. Let $d$ be the actual degree of $E(x,\cdot)$. Then the good part of $Z_d$ is nonempty. Proposition <a href="#prop:candidate-coordinates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:candidate-coordinates">24</a> supplies a point $c^*=(x^*,t^*)$ in that part and highest nonzero coefficient indices $j_i$ for which $P_{i,j_i}(c_i^*)=0$. By Proposition <a href="#prop:root-labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:root-labels">14</a>, labels can be chosen to single out each $c_i^*$ among the real roots of its coefficient polynomial. For these choices, <a href="#eq:selected-coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selected-coordinates">[eq:selected-coordinates]</a> has exactly the one solution $c^*$. It satisfies <a href="#eq:final-nonempty" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-nonempty">[eq:final-nonempty]</a>, and goodness of $x^*$ gives <a href="#eq:final-no-counterexample" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-no-counterexample">[eq:final-no-counterexample]</a>. Some discrete choices therefore accept. Notice that the decision procedure does not need to verify that an index is highest or a coefficient polynomial nonzero: those properties provide complete choices, whereas the two tests ensure soundness for all choices.

Finally, all discrete choices together have polynomial encoding length. There are only $r+1$ coefficient indices and labels; each degree bound, index bound, and label bound is short. The real variables and the number of equations and positivity tests in <a href="#eq:final-nonempty" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-nonempty">[eq:final-nonempty]</a>–<a href="#eq:final-no-counterexample" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-no-counterexample">[eq:final-no-counterexample]</a> are polynomially bounded. The controlled-polynomial rules are used in a fixed order: construct $Q$ and $E$, extract their coefficients and form $W_d$, construct the coordinate polynomials, form the labels, and apply the two existential tests. Each stage is uniform in its short indices; none introduces a separate oracle or one counting level per index. The existential quantification over the entire discrete choice tuple thus preserves availability. The resulting decision function belongs to a fixed level of the counting hierarchy, independent of the input. Malformed inputs were rejected in polynomial time, so this proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="sec:existential-bound"></a>
### An explicit level for the existential fragment

For an ordinary existential sentence, the polynomial to be tested is explicitly listed after the quartic reduction. This permits more precise level accounting than the controlled-family formulation requires. There is also a simpler root selection: we need to isolate some root below a threshold, whereas the two-block argument needs to specify any prescribed coordinate root. The simpler selection yields the following refinement.

<a id="stmt-thm-etr-bound"></a>
<div id="thm:etr-bound" class="theorem">

**Theorem 25**. *For the circuit encoding of the existential theory of the reals used in this paper, $$\mathsf{ETR}\in\mathsf C_{26}\mathsf P,
 \qquad \exists\mathbb R\subseteq\mathsf C_{26}\mathsf P.$$*

</div>

We prove the bound using the preceding algebraic and counting tools, with a short list of derivative conditions in place of the labels from Section <a href="#sec:labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:labels">4</a>.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Selecting some root with few derivative conditions

The distinction between selecting some root and selecting a prescribed root is elementary but useful. Derivative signs distinguish all real roots by Lemma <a href="#lem:thom-separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:thom-separation">16</a>. If only one root from a nonempty set is needed, repeatedly keeping a smallest nonempty sign class gives a much shorter description.

<a id="stmt-lem-sparse-selection"></a>
<div id="lem:sparse-selection" class="lemma">

**Lemma 26** (Sparse root selection). *Let $p\in\mathbb R[y]$ be a nonzero polynomial of degree $d\ge1$, and let $S$ be a nonempty set of distinct real roots of $p$. There is a list of at most $\lceil\log_2|S|\rceil$ conditions $$\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}p^{(j)}(\alpha)=v,
 \qquad 1\le j\le d,\quad v\in\{-1,0,1\},$$ satisfied by exactly one member of $S$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-sparse-selection"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-sparse-selection</summary>

*Proof.* If at least two roots remain, their derivative sign strings differ in some coordinate. That coordinate partitions the remaining roots into at least two nonempty classes. Record the condition defining a smallest nonempty class and keep that class; its size is at most half the previous size. After at most $\lceil\log_2|S|\rceil$ repetitions exactly one root remains. If $|S|=1$, use the empty list. ◻

</details>

For an $\mathsf{ETR}$ instance $\exists y\,\Theta(y)$, apply Lemma <a href="#lem:quartic-fibers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:quartic-fibers">22</a> with no free parameters and with $\Phi=\neg\Theta$. Renaming the full variable tuple gives an explicitly listed nonnegative polynomial $F\in\mathbb Z[x_1,\ldots,x_n]$ with $n\ge1$ and degree at most four, whose real zeros encode truth of the instance. Use Proposition <a href="#prop:specialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:specialization">21</a> with $\ell=5$. In its notation, $D=5^n$, $U=2^\sigma$, $R=2^\rho$, and $$q(y)=\det(yI-M_{h_U})\in\mathbb Z[y]$$ is monic and squarefree of degree $D$. The original sentence is true if and only if $q$ has a real root below $R$. Take $K=\max(1,H+\sigma V)$ from that proposition, so $\left\lVert q\right\rVert_1\le2^K$ and $K$ is short and polynomial-time computable. Although $U$, $R$, and the coefficients of $q$ may have long binary expansions, their bound exponents are short.

Put $T=2+3n$. A test list consists of $t$ pairs $(b_i,v_i)$, where $$1\le t\le T,\qquad b_1(y)=R-y,\qquad v_1=1,$$ and each subsequent $b_i$ is $q^{(j_i)}$ for some $1\le j_i\le D$, with $v_i\in\{-1,0,1\}$. A root matches the list when $\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}b_i(\alpha)=v_i$ for every $i$. If a real root below $R$ exists, Lemma <a href="#lem:sparse-selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sparse-selection">26</a> applied to all such roots gives a list matching exactly one real root. Indeed, at most $\lceil\log_2D\rceil\le3n$ derivative conditions suffice. Every list has polynomial encoding length, since both $T$ and $\log D$ are polynomially bounded.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### A polynomial detecting the list

We next turn these conditions into a polynomial that is negative exactly at matching real roots. The construction is the rational sign approximation of Lemma <a href="#lem:rational-sign" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rational-sign">17</a>, now applied to only polynomially many tests.

<a id="eq:sparse-value-range"></a>
First we need a common range for their nonzero values. Every complex root $\alpha$ of $q$ satisfies $|\alpha|\le2^{K+1}$, and every test polynomial satisfies $$\deg b_i\le D,
 \qquad \left\lVert b_i\right\rVert_1\le2^{K+D^2+\rho+2}.$$ For derivatives, the coefficient increase is bounded by $D^D\le2^{D^2}$; the same displayed estimate covers $R-y$. Set <a id="eq:sparse-value-bounds"></a>$$\label{eq:sparse-value-bounds}
 B_0=K+D^2+\rho+2+D(K+1),\qquad B=DB_0+2.$$ Then $|b_i(\alpha)|\le2^{B_0}$. For the lower bound, the eigenvalues of the integer matrix $b_i(M_{h_U})$ are these values at the $D$ roots of $q$. The product of its nonzero eigenvalues, up to sign, is a nonzero coefficient of its monic integer characteristic polynomial, and so has absolute value at least one. Bounding the other factors by $2^{B_0}$ gives $$\label{eq:sparse-value-range}
 b_i(\alpha)\ne0
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 2^{-B}\le |b_i(\alpha)|\le2^B.$$ This argument permits some test values to vanish.

<a id="eq:sparse-approximant"></a>
Let $s=T+10$ and define $$\label{eq:sparse-approximant}
 P(z)=\prod_{j=-B}^{B}
 \left(2^{\max(0,j)}+2^{\max(0,-j)}z\right)^{2s},
 \qquad
 A(z)=\frac{P(z)-P(-z)}{P(z)+P(-z)}.$$ By Lemma <a href="#lem:rational-sign" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rational-sign">17</a>, the denominator is at least one on the real line, $A(0)=0$, and Equation <a href="#eq:sparse-value-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sparse-value-range">[eq:sparse-value-range]</a> implies $$|A(b_i(\alpha))-\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}b_i(\alpha)|
 \le2\cdot3^{-2s}\le\frac1{8T}
 \qquad(q(\alpha)=0,\ \alpha\in\mathbb R).$$ Consequently $$\Psi(y)=4\sum_{i=1}^{t}(A(b_i(y))-v_i)^2-1$$ is at most $-1+1/(16T)$ at a matching real root. At any other real root one difference in the sum has absolute value at least $1-1/(8T)\ge7/8$, and therefore $\Psi\ge33/16$.

<a id="eq:sparse-product-criterion"></a>
Clear the positive squared denominators by setting $$\begin{aligned}
 D_i(y)&=P(b_i(y))+P(-b_i(y)),\\
 N_i(y)&=P(b_i(y))-P(-b_i(y)),\\
 g_*(y)&=4\sum_{i=1}^{t}(N_i(y)-v_iD_i(y))^2
                 \prod_{j\ne i}D_j(y)^2
              -\prod_{j=1}^{t}D_j(y)^2.
\end{aligned}$$ This is an integer polynomial, equal on the real line to $\Psi\prod_iD_i^2$. As every $D_i\ge1$, it satisfies <a id="eq:sparse-sign-margin"></a>$$\label{eq:sparse-sign-margin}
 \begin{cases}
  g_*(\alpha)\le-\tfrac12,&\text{if $\alpha$ matches the list},\\
  g_*(\alpha)\ge\tfrac12,&\text{otherwise},
 \end{cases}
 \qquad(q(\alpha)=0,\ \alpha\in\mathbb R).$$ For one additional choice $e\in\{0,\ldots,D\}$ put <a id="eq:sparse-product"></a>$$\label{eq:sparse-product}
 g(y)=4Dg_*(y)+e,
 \qquad
 X=\det M_{g(h_U)}
   =\prod_{q(\alpha)=0}g(\alpha)\in\mathbb Z.$$ The determinant identity is Lemma <a href="#lem:real-eigenline" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:real-eigenline">11</a>. The sign argument of Section <a href="#sec:existential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:existential">5</a>, applied to $L=-g_*$, gives $$\label{eq:sparse-product-criterion}
 q\text{ has a real root below }R
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 X<0\text{ for some test list and some }e.$$ Indeed, every $e$ preserves the real-root signs. A list matching exactly one real root gives exactly one negative real factor; some $e$ avoids every nonreal zero, since each of at most $D$ nonreal roots forbids at most one of the $D+1$ choices. The conjugate pairs then contribute positive factors. Conversely, a negative product has a negative real factor, whose root matches the list and hence lies below $R$.

<a id="eq:sparse-size-bounds"></a>
For the subsequent computations we record explicit short bounds. Writing $m_*=2s(2B+1)$ for the degree of $P$, set $$\label{eq:sparse-size-bounds}
 \begin{split}
 d_g&=2Tm_*D,\\
 l_*&=m_*(2B+1),\\
 E_*&=2T(l_*+2)+16(D+T+2),\\
 m_X&=DE_*+1.
 \end{split}$$ Then $\deg g\le d_g$ and $|X|\le2^{m_X}$. For the first bound, every term in $g_*$ has $t$ squared factors, each of degree at most $m_*D$. For the second, at every complex root $\alpha$ each base in $P(\pm b_i(\alpha))$ has modulus at most $2^{2B+1}$. Hence $$|P(\pm b_i(\alpha))|\le2^{l_*},\qquad
 |D_i(\alpha)|,|N_i(\alpha)|\le2^{l_*+1},\qquad
 |N_i(\alpha)-v_iD_i(\alpha)|\le2^{l_*+2}.$$ Therefore $$|g(\alpha)|\le D(16T+5)\,2^{2T(l_*+2)}\le2^{E_*},$$ which gives the asserted product bound. All displayed bound parameters use a fixed number of sums and products of short integers, so are themselves short and polynomial-time computable.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Accounting for the oracle levels

The two multiplication-determinant computations now have an explicit cost. Set $$\Omega=6(1+d_g),\qquad L_0=D\Omega+4n,\qquad
 \lambda=5L_0+4n+D^2+10.$$ For every prime $p>\lambda$, Proposition <a href="#prop:char-coeff" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:char-coeff">12</a> applies both to $h_U$, of degree at most six, and to $g(h_U)$, of degree at most $6d_g$. The larger cutoff is used for both applications. To recover $\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}X$ we need only $p\le Y=2\max(16,m_X,\lambda)^4$, as in Proposition <a href="#prop:crt-sign" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:crt-sign">7</a>. In particular every queried prime has polynomial bit length.

For the first application, the relations $f_i(\cdot,U)$ and the objective $h_U$ have direct polynomial-bit-time evaluations modulo $p$. Indeed, $\ell=5$ gives $a_i=20^i$, with polynomial bit length. The quartic $F$ and its first and second derivatives have polynomial-size monomial lists. The constants $U\bmod p$ and $R\bmod p$ are computed by powering with the short exponents $\sigma$ and $\rho$. The Jacobian is an $n\times n$ determinant of directly evaluable entries. Thus Proposition <a href="#prop:char-coeff" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:char-coeff">12</a>, with $a=0$, computes the indexed coefficients $q_k\bmod p$ of $q(y)=\sum_{k=0}^{D}q_ky^k$ in $\mathsf F_{8}$.

For $1\le j\le D$, $$q^{(j)}(y)=\sum_{k=j}^{D}q_k
       \left(\prod_{r=0}^{j-1}(k-r)\right)y^{k-j}.$$ The falling factorial is in $\mathsf F_{4}$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:product">3</a>; the outer sum therefore gives derivative evaluations in $\mathsf F_{9}$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:counting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:counting">2</a>. This includes every test $b_i$, since $R-y$ is directly evaluable. Each factor of $P(b_i(y))$ in Equation <a href="#eq:sparse-approximant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sparse-approximant">[eq:sparse-approximant]</a> is consequently in $\mathsf F_{9}$. The product lemma puts $P(\pm b_i(y))$ in $\mathsf F_{13}$. Since the list contains only polynomially many tests, the formulas for $D_i,N_i,g_*$, and $g$ need only polynomially many such calls and short arithmetic operations. Thus $g(y)$, and then $g(h_U(x))$, are evaluable in $\mathsf F_{13}$. These expressions use only integer additions and multiplications; their field evaluations never invert a test denominator.

The second application of Proposition <a href="#prop:char-coeff" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:char-coeff">12</a>, with $a=13$ and $v=g(h_U)$, computes $$X\bmod p=(-1)^D[y^0]\det(yI-M_{g(h_U)})\bmod p$$ in $\mathsf F_{21}$. The first application uses only the original quartic and its critical equations; the second calls the first through the evaluations of $g$. This is a fixed sequence of two applications, independent of $n$ and $D$.

By Proposition <a href="#prop:crt-sign" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:crt-sign">7</a> and Equation <a href="#eq:sparse-size-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sparse-size-bounds">[eq:sparse-size-bounds]</a>, the sign of $X$ is in $\mathsf F_{24}$. Lemma <a href="#lem:counting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:counting">2</a> then puts existence of a test list and an $e$ with $X<0$ in $\mathsf F_{25}$. Equation <a href="#eq:sparse-product-criterion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sparse-product-criterion">[eq:sparse-product-criterion]</a> therefore gives a decision function for $\mathsf{ETR}$ at that level, and a language in $\mathsf C_{26}\mathsf P$. Polynomial-time many-one precomputation preserves this level, proving the second assertion of Theorem <a href="#thm:etr-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:etr-bound">25</a>.

<div id="tab:existential-levels">

| Quantity or decision                   |     Sufficient class      |
|:---------------------------------------|:-------------------------:|
| Indexed coefficients of $q\bmod p$     |      $\mathsf F_{8}$      |
| Derivative-test evaluations            |      $\mathsf F_{9}$      |
| Evaluations of $P$, $g$, and $g(h_U)$  |     $\mathsf F_{13}$      |
| $X\bmod p$                             |     $\mathsf F_{21}$      |
| $\mathop{\mathrm{sgn}}X$               |     $\mathsf F_{24}$      |
| Existence of a list and $e$ with $X<0$ |     $\mathsf F_{25}$      |
| Membership in $\mathsf{ETR}$           | $\mathsf C_{26}\mathsf P$ |

A conservative level bound for the existential fragment. Every indexed family uses a single uniform evaluator.

</div>

The shortness of all indices is independent of these level counts. The bounds $D,K,\rho,\sigma,B,d_g,m_X,\lambda,Y$ are computed in polynomial bit time, test lists have at most $T$ entries, and every field element and product index has a polynomial-length encoding. Thus no long integer is passed as an explicit oracle query, and Table <a href="#tab:existential-levels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:existential-levels">1</a> describes fixed uniform machines.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### References

<div id="refs" class="references csl-bib-body hanging-indent">

</div>

<div id="ref-AndrewsGargSchost2026" class="csl-entry">

Andrews, Robert, Abhibhav Garg, and Éric Schost. 2026. “Hilbert’s Nullstellensatz Is in the Counting Hierarchy.” arXiv:2602.17904. <https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17904>.

</div>

<div id="ref-BalajiShirmohammadiTavenasWorrell2026" class="csl-entry">

Balaji, Nikhil, Mahsa Shirmohammadi, Sébastien Tavenas, and James Worrell. 2026. “Approximate Polynomial Satisfiability Is in the Counting Hierarchy.” arXiv:2610.00644. <https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00644>.

      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=10.6cm,y=1cm,>=stealth,font=\\small]\n \\fill[blue!12] (0,.38) rectangle (0.333333,.7);\n \\fill[orange!18] (0.666667,.38) rectangle (1,.7);\n \\draw[blue!65!black,thick] (0,.54)--(0.333333,.54);\n \\draw[orange!80!black,thick] (0.666667,.54)--(1,.54);\n \\node[blue!65!black,above] at (.166667,.7) {$X>0$};\n \\node[orange!80!black,above] at (.833333,.7) {$X<0$};\n \\draw[->] (-.025,0)--(1.025,0);\n \\foreach \\x/\\lab in {0/0,0.125/{\\frac18},0.333333/{\\frac13},0.5/{\\frac12},0.666667/{\\frac23},0.875/{\\frac78},1/1}\n  {\\draw (\\x,.045)--(\\x,-.045);\\node[below] at (\\x,-.06) {$\\lab$};}\n \\draw[black!65,line width=2pt] (.125,-.75)--(.875,-.75);\n \\node[below] at (.5,-.82) {flagging window for $w_k$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A horizontal number line from 0 to 1 with tick marks labeled 0, 1/8, 1/3, 1/2, 2/3, 7/8, and 1. Above the line, a blue line segment spanning [0, 1/3] is labeled \"$X > 0$\", and an orange line segment spanning [2/3, 1] is labeled \"$X < 0$\". Below the line, a horizontal bracket spanning from 1/8 to 7/8 is labeled \"flagging window for $w_k$\"."

(3.2,1.65);\n \\draw[very thick,blue!65!black] (3.8,2.25) .. controls (4.4,2.7)\n   and (4.8,1.8) .. (5.3,2.1);\n \\node[text=blue!65!black] at (2,2.7) {$S_g=C\\cap K_0$};\n \\node[anchor=east] at (-.4,2.05) {$G=M$};\n \\draw[->] (-.35,2.05) -- (.03,2.05);\n \\node at (4.7,3.8) {$\\operatorname{dist}(c,S_g)=\\epsilon$};\n \\draw[->] (4.7,3.57) -- (4.7,3.3);\n \\draw[very thick,black!65] (7.3,1.15) .. controls (8.1,1.6)\n   and (8.3,2.9) .. (9.1,2.65);\n \\node[align=center] at (8.1,.6) {$S_b=(Z\\setminus C)\\cap K_0$};\n \\fill[blue!65!black] (3.05,1.6) circle (2pt);\n \\node[below] at (3.05,1.6) {$c^*$};\n \\fill (3.35,2.55) circle (1.4pt);\n \\fill (3.24,2.17) circle (1.4pt);\n \\fill (3.14,1.88) circle (1.4pt);\n \\draw[->] (3.8,2.6) -- (3.42,2.55);\n \\node[anchor=west] at (3.8,2.6) {$c^\\nu$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A schematic geometric diagram showing an outer rounded region labeled $K_0 = \\{G \\le M\\}$ with a horizontal boundary line labeled $G = M$ on the left. Inside $K$, a blue curved ribbon is labeled $S_g = C \\cap K_0$, surrounded by a dashed boundary line labeled $\\mathrm{dist}(c, S_g) = \\epsilon$.
